=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are going to create N sequences of length 3, satisfying the following conditions.

- For each of k = 1,2,3, the following holds:
- Among the k-th elements of the sequences, each integer from 1 through N appears exactly once.



For this sequence of sequences, define sequences a=(a_1,a_2,\ldots,a_N) and b=(b_1,b_2,\ldots,b_N) as follows.

- Let s_i be the i-th sequence, and let t_i be the reverse of the i-th sequence. When all of these are sorted in lexicographical order, s_i comes a_i-th, and t_i comes b_i-th.
- Here, if there are identical sequences among the 2N sequences, a and b are not defined.

Therefore, if a and b are defined, each integer from 1 through 2N appears exactly once in the concatenation of a and b.
You are given sequences A and B of length N, where each element of A is an integer between 1 and 2N, and each element of B is either an integer between 1 and 2N or -1.
Also, in the concatenation of A and B, each integer other than -1 appears at most once.
How many pairs of sequences a,b are there such that a and b are defined and the following holds for each integer i from 1 through N?

- a_i = A_i.
- b_i = B_i if B_i \neq -1.

Find the count modulo 998244353.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
A_1 A_2 \ldots A_N
B_1 B_2 \ldots B_N

Output

Print the count modulo 998244353.

Constraints


- 2 \leq N \leq 3000
- 1 \leq A_i \leq 2N
- 1 \leq B_i \leq 2N or B_i = -1.
- In the concatenation of A and B, each integer other than -1 appears at most once. That is,
- A_i \neq A_j if i \neq j.
- B_i \neq B_j if i \neq j and B_i,B_j \neq -1.
- A_i \neq B_j.


- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

3
2 3 6
-1 1 -1

Sample Output 1

1

For example, consider creating the following three sequences:

- (1,2,3)
- (2,1,1)
- (3,3,2)

In this case, when sorting s_i and t_i lexicographically, we have:

t_2 = (1,1,2) < s_1 = (1,2,3) < s_2 = (2,1,1) < t_3 = (2,3,3) < t_1 = (3,2,1) < s_3 = (3,3,2)

Thus, (a_1,a_2,a_3,b_1,b_2,b_3) = (2,3,6,5,1,4). Here, a matches the given A, and the second element of b also matches that of B, so this is one pair of sequences a,b satisfying the conditions.
On the other hand, if we create the following three sequences, s_1 and t_1 become identical, so a and b are not defined.

- (1,2,1)
- (2,1,3)
- (3,3,2)

In fact, a=(2,3,6), b=(5,1,4) is the only pair of sequences satisfying the conditions.

Sample Input 2

15
5 16 1 12 30 20 4 13 9 8 24 21 26 28 17
-1 -1 6 -1 -1 -1 -1 -1 -1 -1 -1 29 -1 -1 -1

Sample Output 2

758094847

Print the count modulo 998244353.

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Let's define the sign pattern around the cycle as a binary string of length L (s_j at tail of edge j). The orientation of edge j is forward if s_j=0, backward if s_j=1. Acyclic iff not all s_j equal. The number of linear extensions c(s) is the number of permutations of vertices such that the cyclic order of values has ascents/descents matching? Let's map to values.

Let the linear extension assign values x_j to vertex v_j (1..L). For each edge j (v_j -> v_{j+1}), orientation:
- if s_j=0: x_j < x_{j+1} (ascent from j to j+1)
- if s_j=1: x_{j+1} < x_j (descent from j to j+1)
Thus the cyclic sequence x_1,x_2,...,x_L has ascents/descents exactly: s_j=0 means x_j < x_{j+1}; s_j=1 means x_j > x_{j+1}. So s_j is the descent indicator at position j (descent if 1) for the cyclic sequence. We need count permutations of {1..L} assigned to positions 1..L such that the cyclic up/down pattern is exactly s. This is the number of cyclic permutations with a given descent set. This is known as "cyclic descent number" and can be computed by inclusion-exclusion:
\[
c(s) = \sum_{k=0}^{L-1} (-1)^k \sum_{?} \binom{L}{?}
\]
Actually for linear descent sets, number of permutations with descent set S is given by the "descent set" inclusion-exclusion using Eulerian polynomials. For cyclic descent sets, there is a formula:
\[
c(s) = \sum_{d=0}^{L-1} (-1)^d \binom{L}{d} (L-d)! [\text{condition?}]
\]
Let's derive.

We want count of cyclic orders (permutations of L distinct labels) with descents at specified positions. This is equivalent to number of permutations π of [L] (values at positions 1..L) such that π_i > π_{i+1} iff s_i=1 for all i mod L. This is a "circular permutation" with a given descent set. There is a known formula using the transfer-matrix / inclusion-exclusion over "runs": The number of permutations of [L] with a given cyclic descent set equals
\[
c(s) = \sum_{T \subseteq [L]} (-1)^{|T|} \binom{L}{|T|} (L-|T|)! \cdot \mathbf{1}[\text{...}]
\]
Let's derive from scratch.

Consider the L inequalities: for each i, if s_i=0 then π_i < π_{i+1}; if s_i=1 then π_i > π_{i+1}. This is a system of strict inequalities on a cycle. We can count integer assignments π_i ∈ {1..L} distinct satisfying them. Since values are a permutation, this is the number of linear extensions.

We can use the "order polytope" or inclusion-exclusion over equalities? Another approach: The number of permutations with a given descent set on a circle can be computed by the "cycle lemma" from linear descent sets. For any linear descent set S on positions 1..L-1 (ignoring cyclic edge), the number of permutations with those descents is known. The cyclic condition adds a constraint on the last edge. We can sum over the value at position 1 maybe.

Maybe there is a simpler formula for the sum over cycles in our DP that avoids computing c(s) explicitly. Since the sign pattern s is fixed globally, and the cycles of z are formed by concatenating paths. The weight of a cycle depends on the cyclic binary string of s along the cycle. This is exactly the number of linear extensions of a cycle with that descent pattern. Could compute c(s) for a cycle by a transfer matrix of size O(L^2)? But in DP over paths, we need combine weights of cycles formed from paths. Maybe we can use the r,z' transformation with fixed edges and inclusion-exclusion over excedance set, which might yield a formula involving Eulerian numbers with forbidden positions. Let's explore that route; it may be more tractable.

We need count pairs (r,z) with z extending f and sign constraints. In r,z' terms:
- z' is a permutation of [N].
- E = excedance set of z' must equal r(Z0), where Z0 = {v: s_v=0}.
- For each specified edge c->v, z'_{r(c)} = r(v).

Let's denote the partial permutation f on vertices. We can think of choosing r and z'. Maybe we can sum over z' first? For a fixed z', the condition E = r(Z0) means r maps Z0 to E. Also fixed entries impose r(v) = z'_{r(c)} for each edge c->v. This is a set of equations on r. For a fixed z', count r satisfying:
1. r is a bijection V->[N].
2. r(Z0)=E (excedance set of z').
3. For each c∈C, r(f(c)) = z'_{r(c)}.

This is like counting bijections with constraints. Not obviously easier.

Maybe use inclusion-exclusion over the specified edges. Since K could be up to N, but maybe we can use the principle of inclusion-exclusion to remove fixed-edge constraints. We want sum over z extending f of c(z). This is the same as:
\[
\sum_{z} c(z) \prod_{(c,v)\in F} [z(c)=v].
\]
We can express [z(c)=v] via inclusion-exclusion over permutations? Or use rook polynomial for partial permutation. Since c(z) factorizes over cycles, and fixed edges are local, the cycle DP over paths seems natural.

Let's develop the path DP. We have a partial permutation f (specified edges). Its directed graph consists of:
- isolated vertices (no specified in/out): paths of length 0.
- directed paths: a sequence of vertices where each has outdegree 1 except last, indegree 1 except first. Length can be 0 for isolated.
- directed cycles: if specified edges form a cycle. But can a specified edge form a cycle? Yes, if B constraints include a cycle. In that case, those vertices are already a full cycle of z; no additional edges can be added to that cycle. If a specified cycle exists, it is a component of z fixed. It contributes a weight c(cycle) and no further completion. Also if a specified cycle has length 1 (c->c) invalid; but we already reject c=v. Length >1 possible.

The partial permutation f may have paths and cycles. Since each vertex has outdegree ≤1 and indegree ≤1, components are paths or cycles. In a completion z, each path must be part of a cycle; cycles are already closed and remain as cycles. We need count ways to arrange the paths into cycles by connecting end of one path to start of another. This is exactly counting permutations of the set of paths with cyclic order, where each path has a start and an end. A cycle in z is formed by taking an ordered cyclic list of paths P_1, P_2, ..., P_k, and adding edges end(P_j) -> start(P_{j+1}) (with P_{k+1}=P_1). The specified edges within each path remain. The resulting z-cycle is the concatenation.

Thus the problem reduces to: Given M paths (including isolated vertices), each path has a sequence of vertices with known s values along internal edges. We need sum over all ways to partition the M paths into cycles and cyclically order each cycle, of the product over cycles of c(cycle), where c(cycle) is the number of linear extensions of the oriented cycle formed by the path internal orientations plus connecting edge orientations.

This is a classic "weighted cycles of labeled objects" problem. If the weight of a cycle depends only on the multiset/sequence of paths in it, we can use exponential generating functions. But M up to N (3000), and paths have types based on their internal sign pattern and start/end signs. The connecting edge orientation depends on the sign of the end vertex of the previous path (s_end). The start vertex's sign affects the first internal edge? Actually the first vertex of a path has no incoming specified edge; in the final cycle, the connecting edge from previous path's end to this start has orientation based on previous end's sign. The start vertex's own sign s_start will determine the orientation of the edge from start to its successor (if path length >0, this is the first specified edge; if path length 0, start=end and there is no internal edge; the vertex's sign determines the orientation of the edge from this vertex to the next path's start? Wait for an isolated vertex (path length 0, start=end=v), in the final cycle, it has one incoming edge from previous end u (z(u)=v) and one outgoing edge v->next start w (z(v)=w). The orientation of the outgoing edge v->w is determined by s_v. The orientation of the incoming edge u->v is determined by s_u. So the vertex's sign s_v is used for the edge from v to the next path's start, not for the incoming edge. Thus when concatenating paths, each path has an "outgoing sign" = s_end (for the connecting edge from its end to next start), and an "incoming" edge orientation determined by previous path's end sign. The internal edges within the path have orientations determined by s of each tail vertex along the path.

So a path can be summarized by:
- a sequence of vertices v_1 (start), v_2, ..., v_l (end), with specified edges v_j -> v_{j+1} for j=1..l-1.
- The internal edge orientations are known: for each j=1..l-1, orientation between v_j and v_{j+1} is forward if s_{v_j}=0, backward if s_{v_j}=1.
- The outgoing connecting edge from v_l to next start has orientation based on s_{v_l}.
- The incoming connecting edge from previous end to v_1 has orientation based on previous end's sign, not on v_1.

Thus when forming a cycle from paths, the oriented cycle is a concatenation of path internal oriented edges and connecting oriented edges. The sign pattern along the cycle (s at each vertex) is actually fixed globally: each vertex v has its own s_v, and the edge from v to its successor in z (whether internal or connecting) has orientation based on s_v. Therefore, if we list the vertices in order along the final z-cycle, the sign pattern is just the sequence of s_v for each vertex in that order. The path decomposition is just a way to group some consecutive vertices whose successor is specified. So a cycle's weight c depends only on the cyclic sequence of s-values along the cycle. This is independent of path boundaries except that the sequence is broken into segments (paths) with known consecutive vertices.

Therefore, the DP over paths can be seen as: We have a set of strings (paths) over alphabet {0,1} (the s-values of vertices in order along z), with the property that within a path, consecutive vertices are linked by specified edges. Additionally, each path has a start vertex and end vertex; the s-values are known. We need to concatenate these strings cyclically into cycles, and each cycle's weight is the number of linear extensions of the oriented cycle with that cyclic binary string. The specified edges are exactly the internal adjacencies within paths; the added edges are between end of one path and start of next, and their orientation is determined by the s-value of the end vertex (which is the last character of the previous path). But in the cyclic binary string, the added edge's orientation is determined by the last character of the previous path, which is the s-value of the vertex before the boundary. So the cyclic binary string is simply the concatenation of the path strings in order. No extra characters. Thus a final cycle corresponds to a cyclic arrangement of some path strings, and its weight is c of the concatenated binary string (cyclic).

So we need sum over all ways to arrange all path strings into cycles, where each cycle's weight is f(concatenated string). This is analogous to counting permutations of multiset of strings with cycle weight depending on the string.

Can we compute this efficiently? The paths are determined by the specified edges. Their lengths sum to N. There can be up to N paths (if no specified edges, all length 1). The number of distinct path strings may be large but total length N. We need O(N^2) perhaps.

Maybe there is a further simplification: The weight c of a cyclic binary string can be expressed as a coefficient in some generating function, and the sum over arrangements of paths into cycles might be computable by a determinant or matrix tree theorem? Let's think.

Counting permutations with cycle weights where the weight of a cycle is a function of the sequence of items is exactly the exponential formula. If items are distinguishable and each item has a type (its s-value), and cycle weight depends on the cyclic sequence of types, then the EGF is
\[
\exp\left(\sum_{\text{cycles } C} w(C) \frac{x^{|C|}}{|C|!}\right)
\]
for labeled atoms. But here atoms are paths, not individual vertices? Actually paths are distinguishable (vertices labels). A cycle is a cyclic sequence of paths. The weight depends on the concatenated s-string, which includes all vertices in the paths. Since paths are distinguishable, we can use labeled combinatorial species: The set of paths are atoms; a cycle is a cyclic sequence of paths. The weight of a cycle is a function of the sequence of path strings. This is not simply a function of the number of atoms and their types because the internal order of s-values within each path matters and the boundary between paths affects the cyclic string but not the weight beyond concatenation. However, since the weight of a cycle depends on the entire cyclic string of s-values, and paths are just segments of that string, we can think of arranging the path segments in a circle. This is equivalent to counting permutations of the multiset of path segments with a weight on the resulting circular binary string. First, derive formula for c(s) for a cyclic binary string s.

We have a cycle of L vertices in order. The poset is an oriented cycle. Number of linear extensions c(s). There is a known formula:
\[
c(s) = \sum_{i=1}^{L} \prod_{j=1}^{L-1} \binom{?}{?}
\]
Let's derive using the "source deletion" recurrence.

Let the sign sequence s_1,...,s_L (where s_i is for edge v_i -> v_{i+1}). For a linear extension, the minimum vertex must be a source: s_{i-1}=1, s_i=0 (indices mod L). Suppose source at v_i. Remove v_i; the remaining graph is a path on vertices v_{i+1}, v_{i+2}, ..., v_{i-1} (in order). The orientations along this path are:
- For edge v_j -> v_{j+1} (j not i-1? actually all edges except the two incident to v_i), orientation is forward if s_j=0, backward if s_j=1.
- The edge v_{i-1}->v_i and v_i->v_{i+1} are removed.
So the remaining poset is an oriented path with a known sequence of signs s_{i+1}, s_{i+2}, ..., s_{i-2}? Wait the edge between v_{i-1} and v_{i-2}? Let's index. The path from v_{i+1} to v_{i-1} following the cycle order: v_{i+1} -> v_{i+2} -> ... -> v_{i-1}. The edges along this path are the original edges except the two incident to v_i. The sign for edge v_j->v_{j+1} is s_j. So the path has vertices in order v_{i+1},...,v_{i-1} and edge signs s_{i+1}, s_{i+2}, ..., s_{i-2} (since edge from v_{i-1} to v_i is removed; edge from v_i to v_{i+1} removed). Thus the path length L-1, with a sign sequence of length L-2? Actually L-1 vertices => L-2 edges. The signs are s_{i+1},...,s_{i-2} (cyclic). Let's denote path sign sequence P of length L-2.

The number of linear extensions of the original poset with minimum at v_i equals the number of linear extensions of this path poset. Because once the minimum is fixed as v_i, the remaining L-1 vertices must respect the path orientations; any linear extension of the path poset combined with v_i first gives a linear extension of the cycle? Since v_i is less than its neighbors (source), the relations v_i < neighbors are satisfied by putting v_i first. There are no other relations involving v_i. So c(cycle) = sum over sources v_i of c_path(sign sequence after removing v_i).

Now, we need c_path for an oriented path with a given sign sequence (length n-1 edges, n vertices). Let's derive formula for c_path.

An oriented path with vertices u_1,...,u_n and edge signs q_1,...,q_{n-1}, where q_j=0 means u_j < u_{j+1} (forward), q_j=1 means u_{j+1} < u_j (backward). This is a poset. Number of linear extensions? This is the number of permutations of n elements with a given descent set on a path (not cyclic). There is a well-known formula using inclusion-exclusion:
For a linear descent set S ⊆ {1,...,n-1}, the number of permutations π_1...π_n with descents exactly at S is
\[
\beta_n(S) = \sum_{T \subseteq S} (-1)^{|S|-|T|} \binom{n}{|T|?}?
\]
Wait, the number of permutations with descent set contained in S (i.e., descents only allowed at S) is the multinomial coefficient:
If descents are allowed only at positions in S, then the permutation is a shuffle of increasing runs determined by the complement of S. Specifically, if we require π_i < π_{i+1} for i∉S (no descent), and allow either at i∈S, then the sequence is a shuffle of the increasing runs: positions are partitioned into blocks of consecutive indices not separated by S? Let's recall.

Number of permutations with descent set exactly S:
Let β_n(S) = # {π: Des(π)=S}.
Then the number with Des(π) ⊆ S is
\[
\alpha_n(S) = \sum_{T \subseteq S} \beta_n(T)
\]
and α_n(S) is the number of permutations whose descents are a subset of S, i.e., π_i < π_{i+1} for all i∉S. This means the sequence is increasing on each maximal consecutive block of positions not containing any element of S? Actually if descents are forbidden at positions not in S, then for i∉S, π_i < π_{i+1}. Thus within each run of consecutive indices not separated by an element of S (i.e., between elements of S), the values are increasing. The blocks are the connected components of the path after cutting at positions in S. If S has elements s_1<...<s_k, then blocks are [1..s_1], [s_1+1..s_2], ..., [s_k+1..n]. The values assigned to each block must be increasing internally. The number of permutations satisfying this is the multinomial coefficient:
\[
\alpha_n(S) = \frac{n!}{\prod_{j} b_j!}
\]
where b_j are block lengths. Because choose which values go to each block (n! / ∏ b_j!), and within each block the increasing order is forced. Then by inclusion-exclusion:
\[
\beta_n(S) = \sum_{T \subseteq S} (-1)^{|S|-|T|} \alpha_n(T).
\]
This is standard.

Our c_path(q) is exactly β_n(D) where D = {j : q_j=1} (descent positions) for a linear sequence. Because q_j=1 means u_j > u_{j+1}, a descent. Yes! So c_path can be computed by inclusion-exclusion over subsets of descent positions.

We can use inclusion-exclusion over the set of descents. Let α_cycle(S) = number of circular permutations whose descent set is a subset of S (i.e., ascents at positions not in S). For a circular permutation, if we forbid descents at positions not in S, then the sequence is increasing along each run between elements of S. On a circle, the blocks form a cycle of increasing runs. The number of circular arrangements with descents only in S is:
\[
\alpha_{\text{cycle}}(S) = \frac{(L-1)!}{\prod b_j!}? 
\]
Let's compute. For a circular permutation, if we cut at a position, the values around the circle are a linear permutation up to rotation. The number of circular permutations (arrangements of L labeled numbers around a circle up to rotation) is (L-1)!. If we require ascents at positions not in S, then the circle is partitioned into blocks of consecutive positions (between elements of S) within which values increase. The number of circular arrangements satisfying this is the number of ways to partition the set {1..L} into blocks of given sizes and arrange the blocks in a circle, with each block internally increasing. Since the blocks are distinguishable by their positions on the circle? Let's be precise.

A circular permutation can be represented as a cyclic sequence of the L numbers. The descent positions are the edges where the next number is smaller. If descents only allowed in S (|S|=k), then the edges not in S are ascents. Thus the circle is divided into k blocks (if k>0) by the descent edges; within each block, numbers are increasing. The blocks themselves are arranged in a circle. The sizes of blocks are determined by the gaps between elements of S. For a fixed set S of descent positions, the block sizes are the lengths of the arcs between consecutive descents. But in a circular permutation, the positions are not labeled? Actually the vertices of the cycle are labeled (v_1,...,v_L), so the descent positions are fixed edges. The circular permutation assigns numbers to these labeled vertices. So the positions are labeled around the cycle. Thus a "circular permutation" here is just a bijection from the L labeled vertices to [L], not modulo rotation. The condition is on the cyclic sequence of values at labeled positions. This is not the usual circular arrangements up to rotation; it's a linear permutation with an extra edge between L and 1. So we can treat it as a linear permutation of length L with a prescribed descent set including possibly position L (edge L->1). The descent set is a subset of [L] (where position i means edge v_i -> v_{i+1}, with v_{L+1}=v_1). Thus it's exactly the same as β_L(S) for a linear permutation of length L with descent set S, except that the "positions" are cyclic and the descent at position L is allowed. Wait a linear permutation of length L has descents at positions 1..L-1 only. But here we have L edges including the wrap-around. However, if we fix the value at a particular vertex, we can linearize.

Maybe we can use the inclusion-exclusion formula for β_L(S) extended with a "circular descent" at position L. There is a known formula for the number of permutations with a given circular descent set:
\[

Let's derive α_cycle(T): number of assignments π_1..π_L (a permutation) such that for all i∉T, π_i < π_{i+1} (with π_{L+1}=π_1). This is the number of permutations whose circular descent set is contained in T. If T has size k. The condition means the circle is divided into k increasing runs (blocks) by the descent edges in T. The number of such permutations is:
- If k=0 (T empty): all edges ascents, so π_1<π_2<...<π_L<π_1 impossible. α=0.
- If k>0: The descent edges in T partition the L positions into k blocks of consecutive vertices. Within each block, values are strictly increasing. The blocks are arranged in a circle; the values within each block are increasing, and the last value of each block is greater than the first value of the next block (descent). The number of ways to assign values to the labeled positions satisfying these block-wise increases is: choose which values go to each block: L! / ∏ b_j! (where b_j are block sizes), and within each block the increasing order is forced. There is no additional choice for arranging blocks because the blocks are fixed in the cyclic order by positions. In both cases, it means that in the final z, z(c)=v. So the path decomposition plus σ must satisfy that the edge from c goes to v. This is a constraint on how paths are arranged and connected.

Maybe we can handle specified edges by modifying the path weights: Since specified edges are fixed, the possible paths are constrained: if a path contains a specified edge c->v, then v must immediately follow c in that path. If a specified edge is cut, then c must be the end of a path and v must be the start of the next path. Thus the specified edges define some mandatory adjacencies in the final z. This is exactly the partial permutation f. In the path decomposition, the specified edges can be seen as "pre-edges" that may be either internal or cut. But the path decomposition formula already sums over all z; to restrict z extending f, we can use inclusion-exclusion over the specified edges? Or adapt the path DP.

Let's think in terms of the partial permutation f. We have a set of specified edges. In any z extending f, these edges are present. In the path decomposition (z,T), each specified edge is either:
- included in a path (if not cut), so it contributes to the path weight as an internal edge with weight w(c) = -1 if s_c=1, +1 if s_c=0.
- cut (if s_c=1 and chosen in T), so it is a connecting edge from the path ending at c to the path starting at v. Since it's a cut, it contributes no internal weight and no sign; but it imposes that c is an end of a path and v is a start of the next path.

If s_c=0, the specified edge cannot be cut, so it must be internal.

Thus the specified edges impose that certain pairs (c,v) are adjacent in z. This is like counting path decompositions of a directed graph where some adjacencies are forced. The forced adjacencies can be contracted! If an edge c->v is forced internal, then c and v must be consecutive in a path; we can merge them into a single "super-vertex" representing the sequence c,v. If a forced edge is cut, then c is end and v is start; but since it's a cut, it connects paths, and in the path decomposition it's not part of a path. However, the cut edge is still a specified edge; in the final connection σ, it must be the edge from the path containing c to the path containing v. This means if we contract forced internal edges, the specified cut edges become mandatory connections between path ends and starts.

But the choice of which specified edges are cut vs internal is part of the inclusion-exclusion sum (T). We need sum over all T⊆S, including choices for specified descent edges. This is more complex but maybe can be handled by considering the partial permutation f and the sign s.

Let's step back. Maybe the original problem has a simpler solution using the r,z' transformation and the specified edges become fixed points in a permutation that can be counted by Eulerian numbers with forbidden positions. \[
(m)! (N-m)! \cdot [x^{N-m}] \prod_{i: B_i=-1} (1 + x + ...)? 
\]
Let's try to derive a direct formula for B constraints using the path decomposition and maybe it simplifies to a determinant.

Let's analyze the partial permutation f more. The specified edges f form a set of directed edges. Since f is a partial injection, its graph is a disjoint union of directed paths and cycles. Let's call these "f-components". In any completion z, each f-component (path or cycle) is contained in a z-cycle. If f has a directed cycle, that entire cycle is fixed and no additional edges can be added to it; it forms a z-cycle by itself. If f has a path, the path will be part of a larger z-cycle, with added edges at the ends.

In the path decomposition (z,T), the specified edges may be cut or internal. But note: If a specified edge is cut, then it is a cut edge in T. Since T is a subset of descent edges, this requires s_c=1. If s_c=0, it must be internal. If s_c=1, it could be either. This means the f-components are not fixed as paths; they can be broken at specified descent edges if those edges are chosen as cuts. However, in the inclusion-exclusion sum, we are summing over all T, so we can choose to cut any subset of specified descent edges. This is equivalent to allowing the f-paths to be further split at specified descent edges. But the specified edges themselves are still present in z; if cut, they connect the resulting pieces.

Maybe we can incorporate the specified edges by treating them as "mandatory edges" in the path decomposition, with the understanding that a mandatory edge can be either internal or a cut. This is similar to counting permutations with some prescribed adjacencies, where each prescribed adjacency has a weight depending on whether it is a cut. We can use the transfer-matrix / inclusion-exclusion over the prescribed edges.

Let's try to formulate the path decomposition with mandatory edges in terms of a weighted count of permutations of "atoms" where the atoms are the connected components of the graph formed by mandatory edges that are forced internal. But since mandatory edges can be cut, we need decide.

and c(C) is the number of cyclic permutations with descent set S. There is a known formula for c(C) in terms of the "run lengths" of the binary string s:

Thus p_l for a path depends only on the number of ones in the path, not on which labels. In the EGF P(x), we sum over all subsets of labels of size l. For a given l, the total p_l over all l-subsets of the N labels is:
\[

\]
where a(U) is the number of s=1 vertices in U. Since p_l depends only on a, and there are \binom{m}{a} \binom{N-m}{l-a} subsets with a ones, where m = total number of s=1 vertices (from A). Thus
\[
P_l = \sum_{a=1}^{\min(l,m)} \binom{m}{a} \binom{N-m}{l-a} a (l-1)! (-1)^{a-1}.
\]
Then
\[
P(x) = \sum_{l=1}^{N} \frac{P_l}{(l!)^2} x^l.
\]
And the total count (without B constraints) would be
\[
\text{Ans} = N! [x^N] \frac{1}{1-P(x)}.
\]
Let's test with N=3, m=1 (s has one 1). Compute P_l:

Now, incorporate B constraints (specified edges f). In the path decomposition, the specified edges are mandatory z-edges. How do they affect the EGF?

We can think of the specified edges as forcing certain adjacencies in the final z. In the path decomposition (z,T), each specified edge c->v is either internal to a path or a cut. If internal, then in the path, v immediately follows c. If cut, then c is the end of a path and v is the start of the next path, and the cut edge connects them. In both cases, the edge c->v is present in z. The path decomposition sum over all z and T can be restricted to z extending f by modifying the path species to account for forced adjacencies.

Maybe we can use inclusion-exclusion over the specified edges to enforce them. Since f is a partial permutation, we can require z(c)=v. The number of z extending f weighted by ∏(c/L!) can be obtained from the unconstrained generating function by "marking" the specified edges and extracting coefficients? There is a known technique: For a permutation, the EGF for permutations with some specified cycles/edges can be obtained by treating the specified edges as "atoms" that must be consecutive.

Let's analyze the specified edges f. They form a partial injection. In the path decomposition, a specified edge can be internal or cut. But note: In the inclusion-exclusion sum over T, the cut edges are exactly the edges in T. T is a subset of descent edges. If a specified edge is a descent (s_c=1), it may be cut; if s_c=0, it cannot be cut. However, in the path decomposition EGF, the cut edges are not part of paths; they are the connections between paths counted by the M! factor. The M! factor counts all permutations of paths, i.e., all ways to connect path ends to path starts. To enforce a specified edge c->v, we need that in the final connection, the successor of c is v. This can happen in two cases:
1. c and v are in the same path, with v immediately after c (internal edge).
2. c is the end of a path and v is the start of the next path (cut edge).

If we think of paths as sequences of vertices, a specified edge c->v means that in the cyclic order of vertices along z, v comes immediately after c. This is a constraint on the cyclic arrangement of paths. In the path decomposition, the paths are linear segments of the z-cycles. The specified edge c->v is an adjacency between two vertices that may be within a path or across a path boundary. If across a boundary, then c must be at the end of a path and v at the start of the next path.

This is exactly the same as counting permutations with prescribed adjacencies. There is a standard way: To enforce a set of adjacencies, we can contract each forced adjacency into a "block" if we know it is internal, but since it could be cut, it's not fixed internal. However, in the inclusion-exclusion sum over T, a specified edge that is cut is in T and thus is a boundary. The choice of whether it is cut is part of the sum. We can handle this by treating each specified edge as a "mandatory adjacency" in the final z, and use the fact that in the path decomposition, the set of cut edges T is a subset of all descent edges. The specified edge can be either in T or not. If it is in T, it contributes to the number of paths M and is a boundary; if not, it is internal.

Maybe we can modify the path species to include specified edges as "glued" adjacencies that cannot be separated, but allow them to be cut with appropriate weight? Let's think.

In the path decomposition, a path is a sequence of vertices connected by non-cut z-edges. The cut edges are the connections between paths. The set of all z-edges is the union of path internal edges and cut edges. If a specified edge is internal, it is part of a path; if cut, it is a connection. In the EGF, the path species P includes all possible internal edges (non-cut) with weight w(tail) (and -1 for s=1). The cut edges are accounted by the M! factor connecting paths, with no weight. To enforce a specified edge c->v, we need to ensure that in the final structure, the edge c->v is either internal or a cut. This is equivalent to saying that the edge c->v is present in the permutation z. In the path decomposition, every z-edge is either internal or cut. So if we sum over all path decompositions and connections, the specified edge is automatically present if we restrict to structures where c's successor is v. This is a constraint on the path decomposition and the connection permutation.

Can we count this by considering the specified edges as "fixed edges" in the permutation, and using the inclusion-exclusion formula for permutations with fixed edges? This suggests we can model specified edges as constraints on the path decomposition and the connection permutation. Perhaps we can use the EGF with "colored" path ends/starts to enforce fixed connections.

Let's define for each path:
- start vertex st(P)
- end vertex en(P) (must have s=1)
The connection permutation σ maps each path P to the next path Q such that z(en(P)) = st(Q). Thus σ is a permutation on paths. A specified edge c->v imposes:
- If c is not the end of a path (i.e., its outgoing edge is internal), then v is the next vertex in the same path.
- If c is the end of a path, then σ(P) = Q where Q is the path starting at v.

So specified edges either fix internal adjacencies within paths or fix entries of σ.

Thus the answer is the number of permutations z (third column) satisfying:

Now, count permutations z with a partial permutation f and no monochromatic cycles. N up to 3000. This is a classic combinatorial DP over the components of f.

Now we need count permutations z extending a partial permutation f, such that each cycle contains both colors (s=0 and s=1). Colors s_v are fixed by A (1 if A in group v is odd, 0 if even). Let's call color c_v ∈ {0,1}. We need no all-0 or all-1 cycles.

The specified edges f from B form a partial permutation. We need count completions to a permutation with no monochromatic cycles.

This is now tractable. Let's analyze the structure of f.

f is a partial injection: each vertex has outdegree ≤1 (if B specifies z_c=v) and indegree ≤1 (because B groups distinct). The directed graph of f consists of:
- isolated vertices (no specified in/out)
- directed paths
- directed cycles (if specified edges form a cycle)

In any completion z, each f-component is contained in a z-cycle. If f has a directed cycle, that cycle is fixed and must be mixed, otherwise answer 0. Also no additional edges can enter/leave it.

If f has a directed path, its start has indegree 0, end has outdegree 0. In a completion, the path is part of a z-cycle. The z-cycle will contain one or more such paths (or isolated vertices) connected by added edges from end of one path to start of the next. This is exactly the same path-cycle construction but now the weight of a cycle is 1 if mixed, 0 if monochromatic. We need count the number of ways to arrange the paths into cycles such that each resulting cycle is mixed.

This is a combinatorial counting problem. We have a set of "segments" (the f-paths). Each segment is a directed path with a sequence of colors. In a final z-cycle, we concatenate segments cyclically: end of segment -> start of next segment. The resulting cycle's color sequence is the concatenation of the segment color sequences. We need every final cycle to contain at least one 0 and one 1. Equivalently, no final cycle is all 0 or all 1.

We need count the number of ways to partition the segments into cycles and cyclically order them, with the added edges determined by segment ends/starts. Since segments are distinguishable by their vertex sets, and each segment has a fixed start and end. A cycle of segments is an ordered cyclic list; the number of ways to arrange a given set of k segments into a cycle is (k-1)!? Let's check: Given k segments, to form a z-cycle, we need connect end of each segment to start of the next. This is a cyclic ordering of the segments. Since segments are labeled, the number of cyclic orders is (k-1)!. However, if some segments have the same start/end? Segments are disjoint and labeled, so (k-1)! for each cycle. But in the permutation of segments σ, a cycle of length k corresponds to (k-1)! cyclic orders? Actually a permutation σ on the k segments has cycles; a specific k-cycle (P1->P2->...->Pk->P1) is one permutation cycle. The number of distinct k-cycles on a given set of k labeled segments is (k-1)!. Yes.

Thus the total count is the number of permutations of the segments (i.e., ways to arrange all segments into cycles) such that each cycle's color multiset is mixed. This is exactly counting permutations of M labeled items (segments) with colors? But each segment has a color sequence, not just a single color. A cycle is mixed iff the concatenation of the segment color sequences contains both colors. Since each segment itself may already contain both colors or be monochromatic.

Let's classify segments by their color content:
- A segment is "mixed" if its internal color sequence contains at least one 0 and one 1. Then any cycle containing this segment is automatically mixed, regardless of other segments.
- A segment is "all 0" (monochromatic 0).
- A segment is "all 1" (monochromatic 1).

If a segment is mixed, it acts as a "good" segment that can be in any cycle and makes it mixed. If a segment is all 0, it can only be in a cycle that also contains at least one all-1 or mixed segment. Similarly all 1.

Thus the problem reduces to counting permutations of M labeled segments, where each segment has a type:
- Type M (mixed): color content has both.
- Type 0: all zeros.
- Type 1: all ones.
We need count permutations of these M segments (as cycles of segments) such that every cycle contains at least one segment of type M, or at least one type0 and one type1. Equivalently, no cycle consists solely of type0 segments, and no cycle consists solely of type1 segments. Cycles containing a mixed segment are always okay.

Is that sufficient? If a cycle contains a mixed segment, it has both colors. Yes. If a cycle contains only type0 and type1 segments, it's mixed iff it has at least one of each. If it contains only type0, bad; only type1, bad. If it contains mixed, good even if also only zeros? Mixed segment itself has both, so good.

Therefore, the count depends only on the numbers:
- a = number of mixed segments
- b = number of all-0 segments
- c = number of all-1 segments
and not on the internal lengths/colors? Wait, if a segment is all-0, its color sequence is all zeros; all-1 all ones; mixed has both. The concatenation of segments in a cycle: if a cycle contains a mixed segment, it's mixed; if it contains all0 and all1, mixed. No other constraints. So yes, only counts matter. But we also need account for the fact that segments are labeled and each segment has a start/end; the number of permutations of segments with cycle structure is just the number of permutations of M labeled items, which is M!, but with the restriction on cycle composition. However, is every permutation of segments realizable as a z permutation? Given segments, a permutation σ on segments defines z by adding edges end(P)->start(σ(P)). This yields a permutation z on vertices. The cycles of σ correspond to cycles of z. Conversely, any z extending f gives a unique σ on segments. Thus the number of z completions equals the number of permutations σ on the M labeled segments such that each cycle satisfies the mixed condition. So we just need count permutations of M labeled items with types (mixed, 0, 1) and no all-0 or all-1 cycles.

This is a standard combinatorial count using exponential generating functions:
- Mixed segments are "safe" and can be in any cycle.
- All-0 segments cannot form a cycle by themselves; they must be in a cycle with at least one non-0 segment (mixed or all-1).
- All-1 segments cannot form a cycle by themselves; they must be in a cycle with at least one non-1 segment (mixed or all-0).

We need count permutations of M labeled items with these cycle restrictions.

Let's denote:
M = number of segments (including isolated vertices) from the partial permutation f.
a = # mixed segments
b = # all-0 segments
c = # all-1 segments
M = a+b+c.

We need count permutations σ on these M labeled segments such that no cycle is composed entirely of type0 and no cycle entirely of type1. Cycles of mixed segments are fine; cycles of mixed+0+1 fine; cycles of 0+1 fine; cycles of mixed+0 fine (mixed has 1); mixed+1 fine.

We can count using inclusion-exclusion over "bad" cycles: cycles consisting only of type0, and cycles consisting only of type1. Since these two bad types are disjoint (a cycle cannot be both all0 and all1 unless empty), we can use the exponential formula for permutations with forbidden cycles.

The EGF for permutations of labeled items where each cycle has a weight depending on its composition is:
\[
F(x_0,x_1,x_m) = \exp\left( \sum_{\text{allowed cycles}} \frac{\text{weight}}{k} \right)
\]
Actually for permutations, the EGF is exp(∑_{cycles} w(C) x^{|C|}/|C|). For labeled items, if a cycle of length k has weight 1 if allowed, 0 if forbidden, the EGF is
\[
\exp\left( \sum_{k\ge1} \frac{1}{k} (x_0^k + x_1^k + x_m^k + \text{mixed combinations?}) \right)
\]
But cycles are sequences of labeled items; the cycle weight for a set of items depends only on the counts of types in the cycle. The standard cycle index for permutations: A cycle of length k containing i items of type0, j of type1, l of typeM (i+j+l=k) has (k-1)! possible cycles on a given set of items? Wait in the EGF for permutations, the contribution of cycles is ∑_{k≥1} (number of cycles on a given k-set) / k! * x^k = ∑ (k-1)!/k! x^k = ∑ x^k/k. So each cycle of length k contributes 1/k times the product of variables for its elements, regardless of composition. Thus the EGF for all permutations is exp(∑_{k≥1} (x_0+x_1+x_m)^k / k) = exp(-log(1 - x_0 - x_1 - x_m)) = 1/(1 - x_0 - x_1 - x_m), and coefficient gives M! (as expected). To forbid certain cycles, we subtract their contributions from the logarithm.

The logarithm of the permutation EGF is the sum over all cycles of (product variables)/k. We want to exclude cycles that are all-0 (i>0,j=l=0) and all-1 (j>0,i=l=0). Cycles containing mixed or both 0 and 1 are allowed. So the allowed cycle log is:
\[
L(x_0,x_1,x_m) = \sum_{k\ge1} \frac{(x_0+x_1+x_m)^k}{k}
- \sum_{k\ge1} \frac{x_0^k}{k}
- \sum_{k\ge1} \frac{x_1^k}{k}
\]
But wait, cycles all-0 and all-1 are included in the first sum; subtract them. Cycles all-mixed? A cycle of only mixed segments is allowed, and is included in first sum. Cycles of only 0 and mixed are allowed (since mixed has 1), included. Cycles of only 1 and mixed allowed. Cycles of only 0 and 1 allowed. So yes, the only forbidden cycles are those with no mixed and only one of the two colors. Thus:
\[
L = -\log(1 - x_0 - x_1 - x_m) + \log(1 - x_0) + \log(1 - x_1)
\]
because -∑ x_0^k/k = log(1-x_0), similarly for x_1.
Then the EGF for allowed permutations is
\[
F = \exp(L) = \frac{(1-x_0)(1-x_1)}{1 - x_0 - x_1 - x_m}.
\]
The number of permutations of M labeled items with a type0, b type1, c typeM is:
\[
\text{Ans} = M! \cdot [x_0^a x_1^b x_m^c] F(x_0,x_1,x_m)
\]
where a = # all-0 segments, b = # all-1 segments, c = # mixed segments? Wait variables: x_0 for all-0 segments, x_1 for all-1, x_m for mixed. Let's set:
- n0 = number of all-0 segments = b
- n1 = number of all-1 segments = c
- nm = number of mixed segments = a
Then count = M! * coeff of x_0^{n0} x_1^{n1} x_m^{nm} in F.

Let's test with simple cases.

Case no mixed, n0=1,n1=1,nm=0, M=2. We need permutations of one 0-segment and one 1-segment. There are 2! =2 permutations of segments. Cycles: either two fixed points (0 cycle all0 bad, 1 cycle all1 bad) invalid; or one 2-cycle containing both, valid. So count=1. Formula: F = (1-x0)(1-x1)/(1-x0-x1). Expand: (1-x0-x1+x0x1)/(1-x0-x1). Coeff x0 x1: Let's compute. 1/(1-x0-x1) = ∑_{i,j} C(i+j,i) x0^i x1^j. Multiply by (1-x0-x1+x0x1). Coeff x0 x1:
from 1 * coeff in 1/(...) = C(2,1)=2.
from -x0 * coeff x1 in 1/(...) = -1.
from -x1 * coeff x0 = -1.
from +x0x1 * coeff 1 = 1.
Total = 2-1-1+1=1. M! =2, coeff=1 => 2? Wait count should be 1. Let's recalc M! * coeff. Coeff of x0 x1 in F is 1. M! =2, gives 2. But actual count is 1. Something off with the EGF coefficient extraction.

Let's check the EGF for permutations. For labeled permutations, the number of permutations with n0,n1,nm items is M! times the coefficient of x0^{n0} x1^{n1} xm^{nm} in F? Let's test with no restrictions (F=1/(1-x0-x1-xm)). Coeff of x0 x1 in 1/(1-x0-x1) is 2. M! =2 => 4, but number of permutations of 2 labeled items is 2, not 4. Ah, the coefficient extraction for labeled permutations is not M! times coefficient; it's M! times coefficient divided by? Let's recall.

For a single variable, the EGF for permutations is exp(∑ x^k/k) = 1/(1-x). The coefficient of x^M is 1. The number of permutations of M labeled items is M!, so the count is M! * [x^M] F(x). Yes, for one variable, [x^M] 1/(1-x)=1, times M! = M!. For two variables, F=1/(1-x0-x1). Coeff x0 x1 = 2 (since 1/(1-x0-x1)=∑_{i,j} C(i+j,i) x0^i x1^j). M! =2, product=4, but actual number of permutations of 2 labeled items with one of each type is 2! =2. Why the discrepancy? Because in the multivariate EGF, the coefficient extraction is not M! times coefficient; it's M! times coefficient divided by ∏ n_i!? Let's derive.

The standard multivariate EGF for labeled structures: If F(x)=∑ f_{n0,n1,nm} x0^{n0} x1^{n1} xm^{nm} / (n0! n1! nm!)? Wait, in labeled combinatorics with multiple types, the EGF is
\[
F(x_0,x_1,x_m) = \sum_{n_0,n_1,n_m} f_{n_0,n_1,n_m} \frac{x_0^{n_0}}{n_0!} \frac{x_1^{n_1}}{n_1!} \frac{x_m^{n_m}}{n_m!}
\]
where f is the number of structures on a set with specified numbers of each type. The ordinary coefficient of x0^{n0} x1^{n1}... is f / (n0! n1! ...). So the count is:
\[
\text{Ans} = n0! n1! nm! \cdot [x_0^{n0} x_1^{n1} x_m^{nm}] F(x_0,x_1,x_m)
\]
not M! times coefficient. For the unrestricted case, F=1/(1-x0-x1). Coeff x0 x1 = 2. n0! n1! =1, gives 2, correct. For one variable, n0! = M!, so M! times coeff works. Thus the correct extraction is multiply by n0! n1! nm!, not M!.

Let's test the forbidden case n0=1,n1=1,nm=0: coeff F=1, n0!n1!=1, answer=1. Correct.

So the formula:
\[
\text{Ans} = n_0! \, n_1! \, n_m! \cdot [x_0^{n_0} x_1^{n_1} x_m^{n_m}] \frac{(1-x_0)(1-x_1)}{1 - x_0 - x_1 - x_m}.
\]

We can simplify this coefficient. Let's denote a = n_m (mixed), b = n_0 (all0), c = n_1 (all1). We need coefficient of x0^b x1^c xm^a in
\[
F = \frac{(1-x0)(1-x1)}{1 - x0 - x1 - xm}.
\]
We can expand:
\[
\frac{1}{1 - x0 - x1 - xm} = \sum_{i,j,k \ge 0} \binom{i+j+k}{i,j,k} x0^i x1^j xm^k
\]
where multinomial = (i+j+k)!/(i!j!k!).
Then multiply by (1 - x0 - x1 + x0 x1).

Coefficient for given (b,c,a):
From 1 * coeff in 1/(...) = C(b+c+a; b,c,a).
From -x0 * coeff at (b-1,c,a) = - C(b-1+c+a; b-1,c,a) if b>=1.
From -x1 * coeff at (b,c-1,a) = - C(b+c-1+a; b,c-1,a) if c>=1.
From +x0 x1 * coeff at (b-1,c-1,a) = + C(b-1+c-1+a; b-1,c-1,a) if b,c>=1.

Thus the coefficient is:
\[
C = \binom{M}{b,c,a} - \binom{M-1}{b-1,c,a}_{b\ge1} - \binom{M-1}{b,c-1,a}_{c\ge1} + \binom{M-2}{b-1,c-1,a}_{b,c\ge1}
\]
where M = a+b+c, and the multinomials with negative entries are 0.

Then answer = b! c! a! * C.

Let's simplify:
First term: M!/(b!c!a!).
Second: if b>=1, (M-1)!/((b-1)! c! a!) = b * (M-1)!/(b! c! a!).
Third: c * (M-1)!/(b! c! a!).
Fourth: if b,c>=1, (M-2)!/((b-1)!(c-1)! a!) = b c * (M-2)!/(b! c! a!).
Multiply by b!c!a!:
\[
\text{Ans} = M! - b (M-1)! - c (M-1)! + b c (M-2)! \quad \text{(with terms zero if counts zero?)}
\]
Wait is that all? Let's check dimensions. If a>0, the terms with a in multinomial: first M!; second (M-1)!; third (M-1)!; fourth (M-2)!. This seems independent of a except through M. Let's test with n0=1,n1=1,nm=0: M=2, b=1,c=1,a=0. Formula: 2! -1*1! -1*1! +1*0! =2-1-1+1=1. Correct.

So the count of valid permutations of segments is simply:
\[

\]
with the convention that if M-2 <0, the last term is 0? But if b,c≥1 then M≥2, so okay. More precisely:

\text{Ans} = M! - (b+c)(M-1)! + bc\,(M-2)! \quad \text{if } M\ge2,

and for M=1: formula gives 1! - (b+c)*0! + bc*(-1)!? invalid. For M=1, b+c=1 (since a=0 or a=1). If segment is mixed (a=1,b=c=0), valid count=1. Now, we need compute M, b, c from the partial permutation f and colors s.

Let's define the specified edges f from B. We need build the directed graph of f. For each vertex v, out[v] = specified successor if any, else -1. in[v] = specified predecessor if any, else -1. Since f is a partial injection, each vertex has indegree/outdegree at most 1.

We need decompose f into components:
- Directed cycles: follow out until back. These are fixed cycles of z. They must be mixed; if any cycle is monochromatic (all s same), answer 0. Also if a cycle length 1 (v->v) invalid, but we already reject B_i with c=v? Actually if B_i specifies z_c=c, then out[c]=c, a self-loop. This is a fixed point, invalid. We should reject if any specified edge c->v with c==v. But also if a specified cycle of length >1 is monochromatic, invalid. If a specified cycle is mixed, it's a valid z-cycle and consumes those vertices; they are not part of the segment permutation. Then M = number of path components (excluding cycles). b = number of all0 paths, c = number of all1 paths, a = number of mixed paths.

So the final algorithm is surprisingly simple. Need ensure the count of a,b pairs equals count of z permutations. Let's prove to ourselves:

Across groups first coordinates differ, so distinct. Good.

Now, implement the decomposition carefully.

Let's define arrays:
N
A[1..N]
B[1..N]

Step 1: Determine c_i and s_i.
c = [0]*(N+1) maybe c_i for original index i.
seen_c = [False]*(N+1)
s = [0]*(N+1) # color of group v, 1 if A_i odd for the i with c_i=v.
For i in 1..N:
  ci = (A[i]+1)//2
  if ci<1 or ci>N: ans=0 (A in 1..2N so ok)
  if seen_c[ci]: invalid
  seen_c[ci]=True
  c[i]=ci
  s[ci] = A[i] & 1  # 1 if odd
After loop, if not all seen_c: invalid. Since N seen, if no duplicate then all true. We can check count.

Step 2: Process B.
out = [-1]*(N+1)
in = [-1]*(N+1)
seen_v = [False]*(N+1)
For i in 1..N:
  if B[i] != -1:
    v = (B[i]+1)//2
    if v<1 or v>N: invalid (B in 1..2N so ok)
    if seen_v[v]: invalid
    seen_v[v]=True
    ci = c[i]
    if ci == v: invalid  # self-loop
    # consistency B[i] == 2*v-1 + s[v]
    if B[i] != 2*v - 1 + s[v]: invalid
    if out[ci] != -1: invalid (shouldn't happen because c_i distinct? Actually c_i distinct, so out[ci] not set before)
    if in[v] != -1: invalid (seen_v ensures v distinct, so in[v] not set)
    out[ci] = v
    in[v] = ci

Note: Could there be a specified edge where ci has out already? Since c_i distinct, no. Could in[v] already set? seen_v distinct, no.

Step 3: Decompose.

visited = [False]*(N+1)
M = b = c = a = 0

First, process paths starting at vertices with in[v] == -1. For each such v:
  if visited[v]: continue (shouldn't)
  path = []
  cur = v
  while cur != -1 and not visited[cur]:
     visited[cur] = True
     path.append(cur)
     cur = out[cur]
  # Since start has in=-1, following out cannot enter a cycle; it should end at -1. But if out leads to a vertex already visited? Could happen if there is a path merging? No indegree ≤1, so no merge. If out leads to a cycle, then the cycle vertex would have indegree from path and from previous cycle vertex, impossible. So cur will be -1.
  # However, if out chain enters a vertex that was visited as part of another path? That would mean a vertex has two incoming? impossible. So safe.
  process_path(path)

Then, process remaining unvisited vertices. They must be in cycles. For each v in 1..N:
  if not visited[v]:
    # start of a cycle
    cycle = []
    cur = v
    while not visited[cur]:
       visited[cur] = True
       cycle.append(cur)
       cur = out[cur]
       if cur == -1: 
          # Shouldn't happen because if in[v]!=-1, out chain cannot end? Actually a vertex with in!=-1 but out=-1 would be end of a path, and its start would have in=-1, so it would have been visited in path processing. Thus remaining unvisited have out!=-1 and form cycles.
          break
    # validate cycle mixed
    process_cycle(cycle)

But wait, what about a vertex with in=-1 but out leads to a vertex with in=-1? That's a path; processed. A vertex with in!=-1 and out=-1 is end of a path; processed from start. A vertex with in!=-1 and out!=-1 could be in a path or cycle. If in path, processed. If cycle, unvisited.

process_path(path):
  M += 1
  has0 = any(s[v]==0 for v in path)
  has1 = any(s[v]==1 for v in path)
  if has0 and has1: a += 1
  elif has0: b += 1
  elif has1: c += 1
  else: impossible (s always 0/1)

process_cycle(cycle):
  has0 = any(s[v]==0 for v in cycle)
  has1 = any(s[v]==1 for v in cycle)
  if not (has0 and has1): invalid (monochromatic cycle)
  # cycle is fixed valid; no M increment.

Need ensure if a specified cycle is found, it is indeed a cycle of out edges. Since out/in within cycle, following out returns to start. Good.

Potential issue: A path of length 1 (isolated vertex) has in=-1,out=-1. It will be processed as a path. M increments. Good.

Now, let's think about the input constraints N up to 3000, O(N) algorithm.

Let's write code:

```python
import sys
MOD = 998244353

def solve():
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    N = int(input())
    A = list(map(int, input().split()))
    B = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    c = [0]*(N+1)
    s = [0]*(N+1)
    seen_c = [False]*(N+1)
    valid = True
    for i in range(N):
        ci = (A[i]+1)//2
        if ci < 1 or ci > N or seen_c[ci]:
            valid = False
        else:
            seen_c[ci] = True
            c[i+1] = ci
            s[ci] = A[i] & 1
    if not valid:
        print(0); return
    # all seen? Since N marks and no duplicate, all true. But if some invalid? We can check count.
    if sum(seen_c) != N:
        print(0); return
    
    out = [-1]*(N+1)
    inn = [-1]*(N+1)
    seen_v = [False]*(N+1)
    for i in range(N):
        if B[i] != -1:
            v = (B[i]+1)//2
            if v < 1 or v > N or seen_v[v]:
                valid = False
                break
            seen_v[v] = True
            ci = c[i+1]
            if ci == v:
                valid = False
                break
            if B[i] != 2*v - 1 + s[v]:
                valid = False
                break
            if out[ci] != -1 or inn[v] != -1:
                valid = False
                break
            out[ci] = v
            inn[v] = ci
    if not valid:
        print(0); return
    
    visited = [False]*(N+1)
    M = b = c = a = 0
    
    def process_path(path):
        nonlocal M, b, c, a
        M += 1
        has0 = has1 = False
        for v in path:
            if s[v] == 0:
                has0 = True
            else:
                has1 = True
        if has0 and has1:
            a += 1
        elif has0:
            b += 1
        else:
            c += 1
    
    def process_cycle(cycle):
        has0 = has1 = False
        for v in cycle:
            if s[v] == 0:
                has0 = True
            else:
                has1 = True
        if not (has0 and has1):
            return False
        return True
    
    # paths starting at in == -1
    for v in range(1, N+1):
        if inn[v] == -1 and not visited[v]:
            path = []
            cur = v
            while cur != -1 and not visited[cur]:
                visited[cur] = True
                path.append(cur)
                cur = out[cur]
            # cur should be -1; if cur != -1, it would mean entered a cycle, impossible. But if visited[cur] True from previous? impossible.
            process_path(path)
    
    # remaining cycles
    for v in range(1, N+1):
        if not visited[v]:
            cycle = []
            cur = v
            while not visited[cur]:
                visited[cur] = True
                cycle.append(cur)
                cur = out[cur]
                if cur == -1:
                    # Shouldn't happen, but if it does, it's a path not caught? Actually if inn[v]!=-1 but out chain ends, then start should have inn=-1? Let's handle gracefully.
                    break
            if not process_cycle(cycle):
                print(0); return
    
    # compute factorials
    fact = [1]*(N+1)
    for i in range(1, N+1):
        fact[i] = fact[i-1]*i % MOD
    
    if M == 0:
        print(1 % MOD)
        return
    
    ans = fact[M] - (b + c) * fact[M-1] % MOD
    ans %= MOD
    if M >= 2:
        ans = (ans + b * c % MOD * fact[M-2]) % MOD
    print(ans % MOD)
```

Need check variable name conflict: c is used for array c_i and also count of all1 paths. In code, I used `c` as list and later `c` count; conflict. Rename count variables: cnt0, cnt1, cntM. Let's adjust.

Also, in path processing, if `cur` becomes -1, loop ends. If `cur` becomes a visited vertex not -1, that would indicate a cycle reachable from a path, which shouldn't happen. But if it happens due to invalid f? f is partial injection, impossible. We can ignore.

Now, let's prove the segment permutation count formula one more time with inclusion-exclusion:
We have M labeled segments. We need count permutations σ of segments such that no cycle is all-0 or all-1. Let A be the set of permutations where some cycle is all-0. Let's re-derive the coefficient with correct factorial extraction to ensure no missing factor.

We had F = (1-x0)(1-x1)/(1-x0-x1-xm).
Coeff C = [x0^{n0} x1^{n1} xm^{nm}] F.
Answer = n0! n1! nm! * C.

We computed C = M!/(n0!n1!nm!) - n0*(M-1)!/(n0!n1!nm!) - n1*(M-1)!/(...) + n0 n1*(M-2)!/(...).
Multiply by n0!n1!nm!:
Answer = M! - n0(M-1)! - n1(M-1)! + n0 n1 (M-2)!.
This matches. Note nm (mixed) doesn't appear except in M. Good.

The order of colors within a mixed path doesn't matter for the mixed condition. Is that always true? A cycle's color set is the union of the color sets of its segments. One more thing: The input format has N then A line then B line. Use sys.stdin.read().split() for simplicity.

Let's code:

```python
import sys

MOD = 998244353

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    N = data[0]
    A = data[1:1+N]
    B = data[1+N:1+2*N]
    
    c = [0]*(N+1)
    s = [0]*(N+1)
    seen_c = [False]*(N+1)
    ok = True
    for i in range(N):
        ci = (A[i] + 1) // 2
        if ci < 1 or ci > N or seen_c[ci]:
            ok = False
            break
        seen_c[ci] = True
        c[i+1] = ci
        s[ci] = A[i] & 1
    if not ok:
        print(0); return
    if sum(seen_c) != N:
        print(0); return
    
    out = [-1]*(N+1)
    inn = [-1]*(N+1)
    seen_v = [False]*(N+1)
    for i in range(N):
        if B[i] != -1:
            v = (B[i] + 1) // 2
            if v < 1 or v > N or seen_v[v]:
                ok = False; break
            seen_v[v] = True
            ci = c[i+1]
            if ci == v:
                ok = False; break
            if B[i] != 2*v - 1 + s[v]:
                ok = False; break
            if out[ci] != -1 or inn[v] != -1:
                ok = False; break
            out[ci] = v
            inn[v] = ci
    if not ok:
        print(0); return
    
    visited = [False]*(N+1)
    M = 0
    cnt0 = 0
    cnt1 = 0
    cntM = 0
    
    def add_path(path):
        nonlocal M, cnt0, cnt1, cntM
        M += 1
        has0 = has1 = False
        for v in path:
            if s[v] == 0:
                has0 = True
            else:
                has1 = True
        if has0 and has1:
            cntM += 1
        elif has0:
            cnt0 += 1
        else:
            cnt1 += 1
    
    def check_cycle(cycle):
        has0 = has1 = False
        for v in cycle:
            if s[v] == 0:
                has0 = True
            else:
                has1 = True
        return has0 and has1
    
    # paths
    for v in range(1, N+1):
        if inn[v] == -1 and not visited[v]:
            path = []
            cur = v
            while cur != -1 and not visited[cur]:
                visited[cur] = True
                path.append(cur)
                cur = out[cur]
            add_path(path)
    
    # cycles
    for v in range(1, N+1):
        if not visited[v]:
            cycle = []
            cur = v
            while not visited[cur]:
                visited[cur] = True
                cycle.append(cur)
                cur = out[cur]
                if cur == -1:
                    break
            if not check_cycle(cycle):
                print(0); return
    
    fact = [1]*(N+1)
    for i in range(1, N+1):
        fact[i] = fact[i-1] * i % MOD
    
    if M == 0:
        print(1)
        return
    
    ans = fact[M] - (cnt0 + cnt1) * fact[M-1] % MOD
    ans %= MOD
    if M >= 2:
        ans = (ans + cnt0 * cnt1 % MOD * fact[M-2]) % MOD
    print(ans % MOD)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Need be absolutely sure that each valid z gives exactly one a,b pair and that a,b pair uniquely determines z. Let's formalize:

This is a cycle with at least one edge in each direction. The directed graph has no directed cycle because to have a directed cycle, all edges would have to be oriented consistently around the cycle (all forward or all backward). Since mixed, acyclic. Acyclic poset has a linear extension. Good.
